  - **Mutation-checked: 28 mutants, 28 caught.** Two survived the first pass and both were real gaps, now closed: a roadblock expiry read one turn late (no test stood a block on its last turn) and handing the chooser a criminal with its knowledge stripped (the known-block test could not tell "routed around it" from "stopped by it", because both leave the criminal where it stood - the recording test now carries non-empty knowledge in its fixture).
  - `bun run verify` passes; 617 tests (was 566), 98 files checked by Biome, zero lint output.

- [x] **M3.8c End conditions and determinism**
  deps: M3.8b-2
  Capture, escape, trust collapse, casualties. **No bankruptcy**: cut at the pre-M2.2 review (decision below), and `GameOutcome`, `balance.score.outcomeBase` and `balance.endConditions` no longer carry it.
  Renumbered in review from M3.8b when the phase loop was split in two. References elsewhere in this file were updated; a stale "M3.8b owes the determinism test" in an older note means this task.
  AC: each end condition has a test that reaches it; determinism property test (seed + action list ⇒ identical final state over 200 random games).
  Notes:
  - **Ticked at M4.3**, on the criterion below. Capture became reachable at M3.11 and `turn.test.ts`'s `ends a hunt the hunter works the checkpoints in, with the criminal taken` reaches it through real play on a hard-coded seed, so "each end condition has a test that reaches it" is now true of all five - `timed_out` arrived at M4.2a with its own. Nothing in this task's own code changed; the AC was met from outside it. The original note follows.
  - **Not ticked at the time: capture is the one criterion not met, and it is blocked on the Inbox.** Escape, trust collapse and casualties are implemented and each has a test that reaches it. Capture has no rule, because the MVP still puts no hunter unit on a node (M3.8b-2's note, and the Inbox entry below that owns the question) - so the AC's "a test that reaches it" is unreachable for it, and writing the branch anyway is what M3.8b-1 and M3.8b-2 both refused to do. **Landing it once the maintainer decides costs one line**: an entry `{ kind: "captured", holds: ... }` at the head of `END_CONDITIONS`, its predicate, the `HAS_A_RULE` row in `endconditions.test.ts` flipped to `true`, and a test that plays whatever new action puts a hunter where the criminal is. Nothing in `turn.ts` changes.
  - **New module `core/src/endconditions.ts`, and the turn loop asks it exactly once.** `END_CONDITIONS` is a `readonly EndConditionEntry[]` - `{ kind, holds }` - in `GameOutcome`'s own declaration order, which is also the precedence order when two hold on one turn (`EVENT_TABLE`'s shape and its ordering argument). An array rather than `validator.ts`'s `Record<Rule, Check>` **because the table is deliberately partial**: a total `Record` would force a `captured` entry that nothing can satisfy, which is the branch this task is not allowed to write, and `Partial<Record<...>>` makes `Object.entries` lie about its value type. The gap is pinned instead of hidden: `HAS_A_RULE` is a `Readonly<Record<EndConditionKind, boolean>>`, so a new `GameOutcome` variant stops the test file compiling until somebody says which side of the line it is on.
  - **`outcomeAfter(world, settings)` is a bare function taking a balance slice**, not a `create*Logic`: it has no collaborators, and `criminalTraversalOf`, `trustFactorOf` and `sightingAccuracy` are the precedent for a pure helper that takes the numbers it reads as an argument. The slice is `EndConditionSettings = { casualtiesToLose, trustCollapseAt }`, which `balance.endConditions` satisfies structurally - no annotation was added to `balance.ts` - and which lets a test vary a threshold without building a whole `Balance`, one small dent in the Inbox's "`Balance` is `typeof BALANCE` so scalars are literal types" entry. `index.ts` exports only `isHuntOver` and `EndConditionKind`: `outcomeAfter` takes a `WorldState` and so is no use outside `core`.
  - **A finished hunt is refused whole: `step` returns `{ kind: "hunt_over", outcome }` and runs no phase.** This is the question M3.8b-1 handed over by name, and it framed it correctly - the question is whether `step` runs at all. Three reasons for refusing rather than letting the phases run and having planning decline the queue: a hunt that had ended would otherwise still file reports, draw events and tick the clock; the end-condition rule would live in two places; and **a finished world has to be a fixed point** or M3.9's replay depends on how many extra steps a caller happens to take. `too_many_actions` is the in-repo precedent for refusing a request without running a phase, and the **queue bound is still checked first** - it is a bound on the request itself, malformed whether or not the world is still running - which a test pins. The outcome rides out in the variant because a bot has no view to read it from, and `GameOutcome` is hunter-visible already.
  - **The check runs last in consequences, after the clock ticks**, which is DESIGN.md's literal order ("meters update, the clock advances, end conditions are checked"). So `outcome.turn` is the **number of turns the hunt lasted**, not the index of its last turn, which is what M3.10 scores as "turns taken" and what M5.6a should print. Moving the check before the tick is a caught mutant.
  - **Escape is "standing on a node in `map.exits`", and deliberately ignores `ExitSchedule`.** No generator emits a `timed` exit yet (M2.1b), so a closed-for-now branch would be unreachable. **M7's timed exits change `hasEscaped` and nothing else.**
  - **The MVP hunt is about five turns long, and that is now visible.** Measured over seeds 1-200 with a hunter who does nothing: **200 of 200 escape, mean 4.66 turns**, none reaching the 24-turn deadline. It was always true; extending play past the exit is what hid it. Two app-wiring tests were pinning behaviour that only existed in that hidden regime and were replaced rather than weakened: `does run out of quiet: pressure reaches its ceiling` needed 30 quiet turns (the ceiling itself is still pinned on a hand-built world, and the real hunt now asserts pressure rises by `pressurePerTurn` for every turn it lasted), and `keeps a full trail behind the criminal` needed 8 (the cap is still pinned on a hand-built world, and the real hunt now asserts one trail entry per turn walked). **M4.3 cannot set a win rate until this is answered** - it is an Inbox entry.
  - **Architecture rule 3's walk exists, and the Inbox half is struck.** `world.test.ts` now carries `offencesIn`, a recursive walker that reports a path per offence: functions, `undefined`, and anything whose prototype is not `Object.prototype`, `Array.prototype` or `null` (which catches `Map`, `Set`, `Date` and every class instance). Bounded at `MAX_STATE_DEPTH = 12`, so a cyclic world fails instead of eating the stack. It runs over **five hunts the game actually played to their end**, over `arbitraryWorld` (replacing the old shallow one-level loop), and over a world with a `Map`, a `Set`, a `RegExp`, a function and an `undefined` planted in it, so the walk is proven able to fail. `Date` could not be the planted class instance: Biome's `noRestrictedGlobals` bans it in `packages/core`, tests included.
  - **The determinism test compares the whole transcript, not the final state.** `turn.slow.test.ts` plays each hunt twice from a generated seed and a generated queue and compares `JSON.stringify` of every `TurnResult` plus the final world; a hunt that diverged and reconverged would pass the weaker check, and the transcript is what M3.9 has to reproduce anyway. 200 runs per the AC, queues built from picks through a small builder table so roadblocks and CCTV are exercised and not only the free briefing. **Measured at 5.8s**, which is over Vitest's 5s default, so the test states `DETERMINISM_TIMEOUT_MS = 30_000` rather than inheriting one. `bun run verify` is 11.8s all told, still well under the 60s trigger for moving anything else.
  - **Mutation-checked: 16 mutants, 16 caught**, no survivors. Covering each predicate's comparison (`<=` to `<`, `>=` to `>`, escape never firing), the table's order and both directions of its membership (an entry dropped, an unreachable `captured` added), the turn stamp, `isHuntOver` inverted, the `hunt_over` guard removed and the two guards swapped, the conditions checked before the clock ticks and not checked at all, the walker made blind three ways, and a module-level counter smuggled into the turn loop - which the slow determinism test catches and which the fast suite catches too, from the "changes nothing but X" tests.
  - `endconditions.ts` and `turn.ts` are both at 100% statements, branches, functions and lines. `core` overall 99.52% statements / 95.9% branches, up from 99.52 / 95.85.
  - `bun run verify` passes; 650 tests (was 617), 101 files checked by Biome, zero lint output.

- [x] **M3.9 Replay format**
  deps: M3.8c
  M3.10 was listed as a dep in the first draft and is not one: the replay format does not score anything. Only M5.6a needs both. Dropped so the two can run in parallel.
  `Replay = { version, seed, setup, actions[] }`, encode to a compact URL-safe string, replay to a sequence of frames. `setup`, not `config`: M3.1a split them so the criminal profile is derived from the seed rather than stored, which keeps the profile out of a shared link (DESIGN.md "After-action replay").
  The original signature `replay(replay) => WorldState[]` cannot be used by M5.6b: architecture rule 4 forbids `apps/web` from importing `WorldState`, and `biome.json` already blocks it by name. DESIGN.md still requires the replay viewer to reveal the criminal's true path. Resolve with a third type declared in M1.2 - a post-game reveal frame carrying the hunter view plus the criminal's true position for that turn, and nothing else. `WorldState[]` may exist inside `core` as an intermediate; it must not cross into `web`.
  Bounded by `LIMITS.maxReplayStringLength` at decode, before parsing or allocating, with an over-the-limit test.
  AC: replaying recorded games reproduces final state exactly; `web` can render a full replay without importing `WorldState`; a replay string one character over the limit is rejected.
  Notes:
  - **Two modules, one concept each: `core/src/replay.ts` is the format and its codec, `core/src/playback.ts` is what running one does.** `replay.ts` is pure data plus two total functions (`encodeReplay`, `decodeReplay`) and holds no collaborators; `playback.ts` is `createPlaybackLogic({ game, turn })`, wired at a composition root like every other unit, because playing a replay is playing the game. Nothing else changed: `turn.ts`, `game.ts` and `view.ts` were not touched, and no new dependency was added.
  - **The grammar is hand-written and URL-safe by construction, not by escaping:** `version.seed.columns.rows.exitCount.maxTurns.difficulty` then `~turn~turn...`, a turn being its actions joined by `_`, an action being a one-character kind code followed by its target id (`cn-1-0`, `re-6-0-h`, `b`). Every character it can produce is RFC 3986 unreserved, so `encodeURIComponent` leaves it untouched and M5.6b's share link needs no second layer - a test asserts `encodeURIComponent(value) === value` for generated replays. **Base64 was not an option**: `core` is built with `types: []` and no DOM lib, so `btoa`, `TextEncoder` and `Buffer` do not exist, and `encodeURIComponent` alone cannot escape a JSON payload into something with a usable separator, because every character it leaves alone can also appear inside an id. The grammar is also about a fifth of the size of escaped JSON and readable in a bug report.
  - **`REPLAY_VERSION = 1`, and a mismatch is refused in both directions, never migrated.** M3.7b's note is the reason: the RNG stream is shared, so the order of `EVENT_TABLE`, of the turn phases or of the fork labels decides what a seed plays, and the same actions against a moved stream are a different hunt. There is nothing to migrate to. `decodeReplay` reads the version **before the header's own field count**, so a string from a later grammar comes back as `unsupported_version` and not as whatever this grammar makes of its bytes; the promise every future version owes is that the first field is the version, in decimal. `makeReplay` is the only way to stamp one. **Bump it whenever anything that moves the stream or the grammar changes.**
  - **`LIMITS.maxReplayStringLength = 8_192`, checked first and on the string itself, before anything is split or allocated.** Tuned on measurement, like M2.3: 500 recorded hunts on the default 8x6 grid at the 24-turn deadline with the three actions a turn can afford encode to a mean of **131 characters** (p50 125, max 466), and the same hunts on a 16x16 grid to a mean of 267 (max 497). The bound is set from the worst hunt that can legitimately exist instead - `maxGameTurns` (240) turns on the largest grid `maxMapNodes` allows (16x16), with every turn's action points spent on that grid's longest ids - which is **7946 characters**, and a test pins that it still encodes. 240 turns of 32 queued actions is 84506 and is refused, correctly: `maxQueuedActions` is an order of magnitude over what a turn can spend, so such a hunt can only be fabricated.
  - **Core's bound and `apps/web`'s should agree, and today both are 8192.** They are two boundaries (a hostile URL versus a malformed replay) but one string, and a link one side accepts and the other refuses is a dead link. Nothing enforces the agreement: `LIMITS` is not exported from `@manhunter/core`, so `web` cannot see it - an Inbox entry asks **M5.6b** to settle that, and until it does the two are kept in step by comment.
  - **Both directions refuse the same set, by construction.** `ReplayRefusal` - `unsupported_version`, `deadline_too_long`, `too_many_turns`, `too_many_actions` - is one union checked by one exported predicate, `replayRefusalIn(replay)`, which `encodeReplay` runs before it builds anything and `decodeReplay` runs on the replay it parsed. So `decode(encode(r))` is either `r` or the same refusal `encode` gave, and a string the encoder produced can never be a link that dies on arrival. `too_long` is deliberately not in that union: it is a fact about a string, so each direction carries its own. **Parsing happens before the count bounds, and that is the point:** the length check is what caps the work (an action costs at least one character), and applying the counts to the parsed replay is what keeps the two directions from drifting apart.
  - **Everything after the length check is still untrusted.** `decodeReplay` returns `{ kind: "malformed", field }` naming which of nine fields was wrong, over a table of 22 hostile strings: a missing field, an extra one, a version that is not a number, a seed in scientific notation or with a sign or past the safe integers, a difficulty with no letter, an action code that means nothing, an action that lost its target, a global action given one, and a target outside the id alphabet. Numbers are matched against `/^\d+$/` and then `Number.isSafeInteger`, ids against `/^[A-Za-z0-9-]+$/`; both patterns are anchored and linear, so no input can make them backtrack. No `any`, no unchecked narrowing except the two documented table lookups (`codecOf` and the reverse code tables), which follow `actions.ts`'s `definitionOf` precedent.
  - **`createPlaybackLogic({ game, turn }).play({ replay, balance })` returns `RevealFrame`s and a `SealedWorld`, never a `WorldState[]`.** One frame for the starting world and one after every turn played, each `{ turn, view, criminalNodeId }` - the hunter's view of that turn plus the criminal's true position, which is exactly DESIGN.md's "true path over the heatmap". `frameOf` is module-private for the same reason `seal` is: a caller that could project a live world could reveal a hunt still running. **M5.6b needs nothing else** - the test's `renderedFrom(frames)` stands in for the UI and takes frames alone - and the final world rides out sealed so M5.6a can score it without playing the replay twice.
  - **A finished world is a fixed point, relied on rather than re-derived.** `step` refuses a settled world whole (M3.8c), so playback stops on `hunt_over` and a replay with queues recorded past the end reaches the same final world *and* the same frames as one that stopped on time - pinned by a test that pads a real recording with three more turns. Trailing queues are dropped rather than refused: they are a longer recording of the same hunt, not a different one.
  - **Each bound is enforced by whoever owns it, and playback forwards the rest with its numbers intact.** `play` checks the two things nothing downstream checks (the version, and more recorded turns than `maxGameTurns`), then lets `create` refuse a deadline or a hopeless map as `not_started` and `step` refuse an over-long queue as `turn_refused` naming the turn. Every variant of `PlaybackResult` is reachable and has a test; none was written for a case that cannot happen.
  - **What a caller has to do to record one:** keep the queue handed to each `step` that returned `{ kind: "turn" }`, in order, and call `makeReplay({ seed, setup, actions })`. Nothing in `core` records for you, because nothing in `core` holds the game between turns. **M5.2's store and M4.2's runner are the two places that will.**
  - **Recorded turns are bounded by `LIMITS.maxGameTurns`, not by `setup.maxTurns`,** because today a hunt has no deadline end condition and genuinely can run past its deadline (the Inbox entry about `GameOutcome` having no timed-out variant). When that variant lands, `replayRefusalIn` should also refuse more recorded turns than the setup allows; there is an Inbox entry.
  - **Mutation-checked: 32 mutants, 31 caught, 1 survivor, and the survivor is equivalent.** Covering the length bound off by one, removed on decode and removed on encode, the constant itself lowered; the version ignored on decode, on encode and in playback, and checked after the grammar instead of before it; the deadline, recorded-turn and per-turn-queue bounds each removed or moved by one; `isWholeCount` letting negatives and unsafe integers through; both regexes unanchored or opened up; the target shape stopping reading `ACTION_TABLE`; two actions and two difficulties sharing a code; the turn separator dropped; an empty queue read as one empty action; a leftover target ignored; the seed read from the wrong field; `makeReplay` stamping the wrong version; and, in playback, the starting frame dropped, the wrong node revealed, the frame taken before the turn instead of after, and a refused turn swallowed. The survivor is `break` to `continue` on `hunt_over`: `step` returns the same refusal however many times it is called, so the two produce byte-identical frames and differ only in wasted iterations - the fixed point is what makes it equivalent, and the padded-replay test pins the behaviour it cannot distinguish.
  - `replay.ts` and `playback.ts` are both at 100% statements, branches, functions and lines. `core` overall 99.59% statements / 96.68% branches, up from 99.59 / 96.03.
  - `bun run verify` passes in 12s; 743 tests (was 667), 107 files checked by Biome, zero lint output. No slow test was added: the whole new suite is 2.8s, and the heaviest part of it is a couple of dozen hunts played for real, so it stays in the fast project.

- [x] **M3.10 Scoring**
  deps: M3.8c, M1.3
  Score a finished game per DESIGN.md "End conditions": turns taken, budget spent, civilian harm, trust remaining, captured alive. `createScoringLogic({ balance }).score(world)`: stateless logic over a finished world returning a typed breakdown. The weights live in `balance.ts` and arrive as an argument, never an import (wiring decision below).
  Added in review: DESIGN.md specifies a score and M5.6a renders a "score breakdown", but nothing in the plan computed one.
  **The breakdown has four components, not five.** Captured-alive was cut at the pre-M2.2 review (decision below) and `capturedAliveBonus` no longer exists in `balance.ts`; it returns with the force actions in M7. Score what the world can vary: turns taken, budget spent, casualties, trust remaining.
  AC: unit tests pin the breakdown for two hand-built finished games; the breakdown is serializable and carries its components, not just a total.
  Notes:
  - **`balance` is an argument, not a factory dep: `createScoringLogic().score({ world, balance })`.** A deliberate departure from the wiring decision above, which still reads `createScoringLogic({ balance }).score(world)`, and the same departure M3.6b made and M3.8a followed: a captured balance is a second copy of the numbers that can silently disagree with the one the turn being scored was played under, and a logic unit's members may only be other logic (AGENTS.md principle 1). Scoring has no collaborators at all, so `createScoringLogic` takes **no** deps - it stays a `create*Logic` rather than becoming a bare function because M5.2's store and M4.2's runner wire it at a composition root like every other unit, and a later component that needs the graph (a distance-to-exit bonus, say) then adds a dep without changing a call site. **The decision text above is stale on two counts** - this signature and "a live capture add" - and is in the Inbox rather than edited here.
  - **`score` takes a `SealedWorld` and unseals internally (M3.1b), so the breakdown is the whole of what leaves.** M5.6a is the consumer and `apps/web` may not name `WorldState` or `unseal` (architecture rule 4, enforced by name in `biome.json`), so a `WorldState` signature would have been unusable by the one task that needs it. **No `biome.json` change was needed**: `ScoreBreakdown` carries the outcome, a base, four `{ kind, measured, points }` components and a total, and none of them mentions the criminal - `GameOutcome` is already hunter-visible through `HunterView`.
  - **Every `GameOutcome` variant scores, table-driven off `balance.score.outcomeBase[outcome.kind]`.** A record lookup rather than a switch, so the `captured` the MVP cannot yet reach (Inbox) needs no branch and no `default`, and `in_progress` scores too rather than being refused - a partial score is what M5.6a would want mid-hunt and what M4.2 gets from a hunt that timed out with no variant to end it (Inbox). A `Readonly<Record<GameOutcome["kind"], GameOutcome>>` in the test stops the file compiling when a variant is added, so the deadline variant cannot land without a base.
  - **The four components are a table too: `SCORE_COMPONENTS`, each `{ kind, measure, weight }` with a signed weight.** Adding M7's captured-alive is a row plus a `ScoreComponentKind`, never a branch, and the direction each component pulls in is written once. The test's `Readonly<Record<ScoreComponentKind, number>>` fails to compile until a new kind says what it is worth, so a fifth component cannot go quietly unrendered.
  - **Turns taken is read off the outcome's stamp, not the clock**, per M3.8c's note that end conditions are checked after the tick, so `outcome.turn` is the number of turns the hunt lasted. A hunt still in progress has no stamp and falls back to `clock.turn`. Both are pinned, including a world whose clock and stamp deliberately disagree.
  - **Budget spent is derived, not recorded: `balance.hunter.startingBudget - hunter.budget`.** Nothing in `WorldState` tracks spend, and nothing needed to until now - planning refuses an action the budget cannot cover (M3.8b-1), so the balance only ever falls. Deliberately unclamped: if a future action is billed after it is chosen (the door the bankruptcy decision names for M7), an overdrawn hunter should score the overdraft rather than have it rounded away.
  - **Each component's points are rounded, not the total, and `-0` is normalised to `0`.** `budgetPenaltyPerUnit` (0.15) is the only non-integer weight, so rounding per component is what makes the receipt M5.6a prints add up to the total beside it. `Math.round(0 * -15)` is `-0`, which survives `JSON.stringify` and which a UI would render with a minus sign, so a component worth nothing is `0`; a test asserts it with `Object.is`.
  - **The total is floored at `balance.score.minimumScore`**, and the floor is visible rather than implied: `base` and `components` carry the unfloored truth, so a breakdown whose components sum below zero still shows what it was made of. Consequence for M4.3, in the Inbox: every trust-collapse loss floors at exactly the minimum.
  - **The invariant `balance.test.ts` asserts from the constants is now also asserted through the scorer itself** - a worst-case capture (full deadline, whole budget, one casualty short of losing, no trust left) scored by `createScoringLogic` outscores a best-case loss scored the same way, 490 to 200. The arithmetic version restates the formula; this one would catch a scorer that implements a different formula from the one the constants were tuned for.
  - **No new balance knob and no `limits.ts` entry.** `outcomeBase`, `turnPenalty`, `budgetPenaltyPerUnit`, `casualtyPenalty`, `trustBonusPerPoint` and `minimumScore` all existed from M1.3 and all six are now consumed; scoring reads a world already in memory and crosses no boundary, so it declares no bound of its own. **M3.8c is still unticked for its capture criterion**, which does not block this task: `captured` worlds are hand-buildable and the capture pin is one of the two the AC asks for.
  - **Mutation-checked: 19 mutants, 19 caught**, no survivors. Each weight's sign, the turn stamp replaced by the clock and the in-progress fallback zeroed, budget spent read as the balance left and subtracted the wrong way round, casualties never counted, trust remaining reading pressure, rounding turned to flooring and removed, the `-0` normalisation dropped, the base pinned to `captured` and reported-but-not-counted and counted-but-not-reported, the floor inverted and removed, and a component dropped from the table.
  - `score.ts` is at 100% statements, branches, functions and lines. `core` overall 99.53% statements / 95.94% branches, up from 99.52 / 95.9.
  - `bun run verify` passes; 667 tests (was 650), 103 files checked by Biome, zero lint output.

- [x] **M3.11 Capture: roadblock interception**
  deps: M3.8b-2, M3.8c
  Added after M3.10, out of numbering order, because it is an MVP turn-loop rule and this is the milestone that owns those. It exists because the maintainer answered the standing Inbox question "the MVP has no reachable capture" (decision below): **a criminal who walks into a roadblock it did not know about is either taken or slips past.** DESIGN.md has been reworded in both places this touches.
  The hook is one branch, `movedCriminal` in `turn.ts`, the one that today records the collision as knowledge and stops the move. It becomes a draw: capture, or the present behaviour.
  **Only an unknown block can catch**, and that is the whole mechanic rather than a detail. A known block is removed from the graph the AI searches (M3.5's `Traversal.blockedEdgeIds` seam, M3.8b-2's note), so the criminal never walks into one - which is what makes the win come from a checkpoint that surprises them and keeps the hunter's job "guess where they are going", not "own every edge".
  **This task gives the turn loop an `rng` dep for the first time.** M3.8a's note says plainly that the loop itself never draws and that the phase order is therefore the whole determinism surface; that stops being true here. Say in the note what the new draw does to the stream and where in the phase it sits, because the answer decides the version bump below. Seven sites wire `createTurnLogic` and all of them need the new dep.
  **`REPLAY_VERSION` must be bumped** (M4.2a just moved it to 2 for a different reason): a new draw moves the RNG stream, which is M3.9's first stated trigger, so every seed plays a different hunt after this lands.
  The slip-past chance is a named knob in `balance.ts`. Pick a defensible first guess and leave the tuning to M4.3, which is what it is for.
  Capture already exists as a `GameOutcome` variant with no rule; landing it is the `{ kind: "captured", holds: ... }` entry at the head of `END_CONDITIONS` that M3.8c built the table to accept, plus whatever plain field on the world that predicate reads. `HAS_A_RULE` in `endconditions.test.ts` flips, and the table becomes total.
  AC: a test reaches `captured` through real play; a criminal that already knows about a block never walks into it and so can never be caught by it; the draw is made once per collision and the same seed plus the same queue still plays the same hunt (M3.8c's determinism test must still pass); `balance.test.ts`'s "worst capture outscores best loss" invariant still holds.
  Notes:
  - The mechanic is a draw per *surprise*, not per roadblock standing. A criminal that tries to cross an edge carrying a block it did not already know about is taken or gets through; a block it knows about is routed around by the AI and never reaches a draw. This needed no new knowledge check in the turn loop: `criminalTraversalOf` already puts `knowledge.knownRoadblockEdgeIds` into the `Traversal` the AI searches, so `edgeTaken` returns `null` for a known-blocked edge and the `blockedEdgeIdsAt(...).has(edgeId)` branch M3.8b-2 left behind is reachable only for blocks the criminal did not know about. That is structural, and `turn.test.ts` asserts it against the real chooser with a stream that would take the criminal every time.
  - Slipping past is unchanged from before this task: stopped short, move not made, block now known. Both branches record the block in `knowledge`, so M3.8b-2's knowledge tests stay independent of the draw.
  - **Where the draw sits, and what it does to the stream.** `TurnDeps` gained `rng` (first member; seven wiring sites updated: `apps/web/src/main.tsx`, `apps/web/src/gamestore.test.ts`, `apps/web/src/storecontext.test.tsx`, `packages/core/src/playback.test.ts`, `packages/core/src/world.test.ts`, `packages/core/src/turn.slow.test.ts`, `packages/sim/src/bots/bots.test.ts`, plus `turn.test.ts`'s own three). The draw is taken inside `intercepted`, in the resolution phase, from the state the criminal's chooser left behind, and the result is threaded back into `world.rng` - one `deps.rng.float` per collision, and none at all on a turn nobody hit anything. It is the turn loop's only draw of its own; every other phase draws through the logic it was handed. This makes the stream depend on whether a collision happened. That is correct and it is deterministic - the same seed and the same queue produce the same collisions and therefore the same draws - but it means two hunts that diverge at a checkpoint diverge in everything drawn afterwards, which is exactly why the replay version moved. `turn.test.ts` pins both halves ("draws exactly once for the collision", "draws nothing at all on a turn nobody hit anything"); M3.8c's determinism test in `turn.slow.test.ts` still passes unchanged.
  - **How the capture is recorded.** `CriminalState.inCustody: boolean`, plain serializable data, set by the resolution phase and read by a new `END_CONDITIONS` entry (`hasBeenCaught`). Not a `WorldState` flag: a world-level field raises the question of whether it is sticky, and the criminal is where the fact belongs. `nextCriminal` spreads `...world.criminal`, so it survives the consequences phase, and `step` refuses a settled world, so nothing can clear it in play. `world.test.ts`'s serializability walker and the JSON round-trip both still pass.
  - `captured` now leads `END_CONDITIONS`, matching `GameOutcome`'s declaration order: a criminal in custody is not at large whatever else that turn did. The table is now total - `endconditions.test.ts`'s `HAS_A_RULE` has no `false` left, and "leaves only the capture the MVP cannot reach without a rule" became "leaves no ending without a rule". DESIGN.md's general co-location rule is still unreachable and still not implemented; this is the checkpoint case only.
  - **The knob**: `BALANCE.actions.roadblock.slipPastChance = 0.6`. The roadblock entry was hoisted out of the inline `BALANCE.actions` literal into an annotated `RoadblockSettings` table, the way `DISTRICTS` / `EDGES` / `PROFILES` / `OUTCOME_BASE` already are, so the field widens to `number` instead of the literal `0.6`; that is what lets a test build a varied `Balance` and what lets M4.3 sweep it. `0.6` is a first guess, measured not derived: over 500 generated hunts, a greedy hunter blocking the roads out of its hottest belief cell captures 63% at 0.6 (75% at 0.4, 50% at 0.7), and an omniscient blockader that closes the roads out of the criminal's true node captures 81%. A passive hunter still captures nothing. The number is set from the hunt rather than from the single collision: a hunter working the criminal's route runs it into several checkpoints before the deadline, so a chance that reads as generous each time it happens still ends a worked hunt in a capture more often than not. **M4.3 owns the real number.** `balance.test.ts` gained "leaves a checkpoint both a way through and a way to take somebody" (strictly inside (0, 1), both ends), and the "worst capture beats the best ending short of one" invariant still holds untouched.
  - **`REPLAY_VERSION` is 3.** The trigger is the stream, not the grammar: every hunt in which a roadblock was ever hit now consumes one number more than it used to, and everything drawn after that collision comes out different. Version 2 recordings of hunts nobody ever blocked would still play, but nothing can tell those apart without playing them both ways. `replay.test.ts` follows M4.2a's pattern - a real version 2 link written out longhand (`2.7.8.6.3.24.s~re-1-0-h~`), refused, plus the version 1 link kept as a second guard, so a reverted bump fails rather than silently replaying someone else's hunt.
  - The AC's "reaches `captured` through real play" is `turn.test.ts`'s "ends a hunt the hunter works the checkpoints in, with the criminal taken", driven by `blockadeAround` - a test-only hunter that reads the criminal's true node and closes the blockable roads out of it. No `HunterView` would allow that; it is the shortest way to drive a whole generated hunt into the ending, and the point being pinned is that the ending is reachable, not that anybody could play that well. All seven `ESCAPE_SEEDS` end `captured` under it. **Stale from M4.3 on**, which moved `slipPastChance` to 0.7: all seven still end with the escape stopped, six end `captured`, and the seventh (`SEED = 7`) ends `timed_out` with the criminal contained for the whole hunt. The seven-seed test was narrowed and the contained hunt promoted into a test of its own - see M4.3's note.
  - DESIGN.md needed no further change; the wording already in the tree matches what was built. Numbers: 843 tests in 47 files pass (`bun run test`, slow project included; 2 files and 7 of those tests belong to the concurrent M4.2b work). 17 tests added by this task. Coverage of the touched core modules is 100% statements/branches/functions/lines for `turn.ts`, `endconditions.ts`, `criminal.ts`, `balance.ts` and `replay.ts`. Mutation check: 17 mutants, 17 killed, 0 survivors - comparison flipped, comparison loosened to `<=`, always-capture, never-capture, knowledge dropped on collision, stream not advanced, draw taken twice, draw taken on every move, chance hard-coded instead of read off the argument, settled stream dropped, capture rule never holds, capture rule removed from the table, capture demoted below the escape, criminal starts in custody, slip chance pinned to 0, slip chance pinned to 1, version bump reverted.

---

## M4 — Headless simulation and balance

There is no M4.0: the belief heatmap moved to **M3.6b** at the pre-M2.3 review, because it is state `WorldState` carries and M3.8a's consequences phase advances, not a post-hoc fold over view history. It was M5.1 before an earlier review.

- [x] **M4.1 Scripted hunter bots**
  deps: M3.8c, M3.2
  `random`, `greedy-roadblock` (block edges on the shortest path to nearest exit), `heatmap-chaser` (over `HunterView.belief`, which M3.6b put there).
  M4.0 is no longer a dep because the distribution now arrives in the view. That is also what keeps the bots stateless, as engineering principle 1 requires: a bot that had to accumulate its own view history would be logic holding data.
  AC: bots only use `HunterView`.
  Notes:
  - **The bots live in `packages/sim/src/bots/`, a directory rather than a file, because that is what makes the acceptance criterion enforceable instead of asserted.** `biome.json` gains one override on `packages/sim/src/bots/**/*.ts` that denies `@manhunter/core`'s hidden-state exports by name - `apps/web`'s twelve plus `SealedWorld`, `RevealFrame` and `createPlaybackLogic` - and `tools/biome/architecture-rules.test.ts` gains two fixtures (`sim_bot_world_state`, `sim_bot_unseal`) that fail the build if the denial ever stops firing. A directory is what lets that test place a fixture inside the guarded path with no change to its `fixturePath` helper; a flat `bots.ts` would have needed one. **Two alternatives were rejected.** Putting the bots in `core` would have made the rule unenforceable: `core` reaches `WorldState` by relative path and `noRestrictedImports` matches package specifiers, so nothing could tell a bot apart from the rules it plays by. Denying the names across `packages/sim` as a whole would contradict the "How `sim` reaches inside a sealed world" decision, which M4.2's runner depends on - **`sim` at large may `unseal`; this one directory may not**, and the `sim_bot_unseal` fixture is the proof that the narrower rule is the one in force. The type-level half is free: `BotRequest` names only the view, the balance and a stream position, so no world of any kind is in scope to reach for.
  - **`createBotLogic({ rng, graph }).plan({ kind, view, balance, state }) => { state, value: HunterAction[] }`.** Deps are logic and only logic; the balance arrives as an argument on every call, per M3.10's note and the two before it, so a bot cannot hold a second copy of the numbers the turn it is planning will be resolved under. **The RNG is threaded state-in, state-out** like `createRng` and `CriminalAiLogic.choose`, which is what makes a seed plus a bot a hunt (architecture rule 2) and is pinned by a 25-run fast-check property over whole hunts.
  - **A bot is a `BotKind` in `BOT_KINDS` and a row in `BOT_TABLE`, never a branch** (architecture rule 6). `BOT_KINDS` is the `as const` array the type is derived from, so it is one list for M4.2's `--bot` to validate against and cannot drift from what exists; `Readonly<Record<BotKind, BotStrategy>>` makes a fourth bot a compile error until it has a strategy. **`BotKind` is snake_case (`greedy_roadblock`, `heatmap_chaser`) like every other discriminant in the repo**, not the plan's hyphenated prose; M4.2 owns the spelling its flag accepts.
  - **A strategy is `(deps, plan, state) => RngDraw<HunterAction | null>` and never sees a queue it built.** One shared driver owns affordability and the loop, and hands each strategy the actions already queued this turn as part of `BotPlan`, so the accumulating queue is data passing through rather than state a bot keeps (engineering principle 1). The driver stops at the first action the strategy cannot name or the hunter cannot pay for: **no bot falls back to a cheaper strategy**, so what M4.3 measures is the strategy and not the fallback.
  - **`greedy_roadblock` reuses `shortestPathToAny` and implements no search**, which is what M1.4a put it in `core` for. The traversal it builds carries `mode: balance.map.escapeMode` and `blockedEdgeIds` = standing containment (`blockedEdgeIdsAt`) plus the edges this turn's queue has already closed, so M3.3's blocked-edge support does the routing-around for free: the second roadblock in a queue is chosen against a map the first one has already altered. **The origin is `map.incidentNodeId`**, the one node both sides know and the node DESIGN.md's amateur walks to its nearest exit from. Rejected: scoring each candidate edge by how much it raises the escape cost - a search per candidate, and further from the plan's wording than the tuning it would buy.
  - **`heatmap_chaser` works the hottest belief cell, canvass then cameras, then moves on.** `HEATMAP_ACTIONS` is a two-entry table, so its length is also how many times one node is worth visiting in a turn, and a node the queue has exhausted drops out of the ranking. Cells of equal mass are separated by a draw rather than by node order, which matters from turn one, where a hunter who has heard nothing believes the city uniformly.
  - **Two new bounds in `packages/sim/src/limits.ts`, both over internal enumeration rather than I/O** (AGENTS.md section 5's last rule). `maxBotCandidates = 2048` caps what one decision may enumerate - the widest legal map is `maxMapNodes` (256) nodes, so the default 8x6 grid's ~160 candidates sit an order of magnitude inside it - and surplus is dropped the way `core`'s `maxAiCandidates` drops its own. `maxBotQueue = 8` caps the queue loop; three action points and a cheapest action of one point is a fact about today's `balance.ts` and M4.3's whole job is to change facts like that. Each has an over-the-cap test that hand-builds a view the shipped generator cannot produce. `limits.test.ts` needed no edit: it already loops `Object.entries(LIMITS)`.
  - **One change in `core`: `actionCostOf(kind, balance)`, two lines over the existing `ACTION_TABLE`, exported.** A bot has to know what an action costs before it queues it, and `ActionLogic.validate` - which answers better - takes a `WorldState` no view-holder can produce. The alternative was a second copy of which balance field each action bills to, in `sim`, which is the copy that drifts. **M5.5 needs the same accessor** to grey out a button before a target is chosen.
  - **Measured over seeds 1-200 on the default 8x6 grid, 24-turn deadline, and no bot produced a single planning rejection in 600 hunts.** Against M3.8c's do-nothing baseline (200 of 200 escaped, mean 4.66 turns, nothing spent, mean score 55.0): `random` escapes 197, spends 517 of 1000, mean 5.80 turns, mean score 2.6. `heatmap_chaser` escapes 200 of 200 at mean 4.65 turns - **buying intel changes nothing about the outcome, which is the capture gap measured from the hunter's side rather than argued**. `greedy_roadblock` is the only bot that moves the game: mean 17.23 turns, 897 of 1000 spent, and only 110 of 200 escapes, with 15 hunts lost to casualties and **75 still in progress at the deadline**. Three Inbox entries asked for exactly this and have been updated with it.
  - **What M4.2 must know.** `packages/sim/src/main.ts` is still a no-op; wiring `createBotLogic` there is M4.2's, with the SVG and artefact units M2.4 left it. The runner has to decide what a hunt still `in_progress` at `setup.maxTurns` counts as, because the greedy bot produces them in bulk - that is the `GameOutcome` deadline-variant entry in the Inbox, and it is reachable today rather than after some future change. Recording a replay needs the queue `plan` returned, in order, which is exactly what `makeReplay` wants (M3.9's note).
  - `bun run verify` passes in 12s; 770 tests (was 743) in 43 files (was 42), 109 files checked by Biome (was 107), zero lint output. `core` coverage is unchanged at 99.59% statements / 96.68% branches - `actionCostOf` is fully covered - and `sim` is not in the coverage scope. The new suite is 1.1s and stays in the fast project; the determinism property runs 25 whole-hunt pairs, against M3.8c's 200 in the `slow` project, because what it pins is the bot and not the engine.

M4.2 is **split as the Inbox required**, into the batch runner and the CLI surface - plus one `core` change in front of both, because the Inbox names M4.2 as the owner of a blocker that neither half can be written around.

- [x] **M4.2a Deadline end condition**
  deps: M4.1
  `GameOutcome` gains the "the deadline ran out" variant the Inbox has tracked since M3.8c, with its `balance.score.outcomeBase` row and one entry in `END_CONDITIONS` reading `world.clock.turn >= world.config.maxTurns`.
  Its own checkbox rather than the head of M4.2b, because it is a `core` change and both halves the Inbox asked for are `sim`. The Inbox entry calls it M4.2's blocker and is measured, not predicted: M4.1 left 75 of 200 greedy hunts `in_progress` at the 24-turn deadline, so a runner that aggregates by outcome kind would report 37% of a greedy batch as still running.
  The M3.9 Inbox entry hands the same task one more line: `replayRefusalIn` should refuse a replay recording more turns than its own `setup.maxTurns`, which after this task is a hunt that could not have happened.
  Precedence is `GameOutcome`'s declaration order (M3.8c). Say in the note where the variant is declared and why - a criminal that reaches an exit on the final turn escaped, it did not run out of clock.
  AC: a test reaches the new outcome through real play, not a hand-built world; `balance.test.ts`'s "worst capture outscores best loss" invariant still holds with the new base inside it; a replay recording more turns than its setup allows is refused; `HAS_A_RULE` in `endconditions.test.ts` records that the table is now total but for `captured`.
  Notes:
  - **`timed_out` is declared last in `GameOutcome`, so it is checked last.** Declaration order is precedence order (M3.8c), and the deadline is the ending that holds when nothing else did: a criminal who reaches an exit on the final turn escaped, and a hunter who loses the last of the public's trust on it was removed from the case - neither ran out of clock. `captured` still leads for the mirror reason. Four tests pin it from the runtime side (`names the escape/trust collapse/casualties ... on the final turn`, plus the order test over `END_CONDITIONS`), because the union's order is type-level and a test cannot read it.
  - **The deadline is the hunt's own `config.maxTurns`, never a balance knob.** It is the number the player's setup named and the number a replay carries, so a hunt judged against anything else could not be replayed - and `outcomeAfter` already takes its thresholds as an argument for M4.3's sweep, which would otherwise have made the deadline sweepable by accident. `reads the deadline off the hunt's own config rather than the balance` is the test that holds the line, and the two real-play tests below would fail with it too.
  - **Checked after the clock has ticked**, which the consequences phase already did (M3.8a), so a hunt with a deadline of `n` gets exactly `n` turns and the stamp reads `n`. `>=` rather than `>`, so a world whose clock somehow ran past its deadline still ends rather than running for ever; a test pins each side of that.
  - **`outcomeBase.timed_out` is 150, a stalemate and not a loss.** DESIGN.md's loss list is escape, trust collapse and casualties, and the clock is on none of them - the criminal is still in the city and the hunter still has its case. The number is bounded from both sides rather than guessed: it sits below `trustMax * trustBonusPerPoint` (200), so a hunt that ran out of clock cannot outscore what the hunter did during it, and below the worst possible capture (490) with the full trust bonus added, so `makes the worst capture beat the best ending short of one` still holds. The hard ceiling the existing arithmetic allows is 290. **M4.3 owns the real number**; this is a defensible first guess.
  - **`balance.test.ts`'s invariant now runs over every `outcomeBase` row rather than over `escaped` alone**, which is what the AC asked for: `Math.max` of every base but `captured` plus the best possible trust bonus, against the worst possible capture. `score.test.ts`'s copy of the invariant was widened the same way, over its `Readonly<Record<GameOutcome["kind"], GameOutcome>>` table, so a sixth ending cannot be added outside either.
  - **`REPLAY_VERSION` is bumped to 2, and neither of M3.9's two stated triggers fired.** The grammar did not move: the header is the same six fields and the turn separator is unchanged. The RNG stream did not move either: no phase draws differently and `step` consumes the same draws in the same order. What moved is the rule - a version 1 recording of a hunt that reaches its own `setup.maxTurns` now ends `timed_out` where it used to end `in_progress`, which is the same seed and the same queues producing a different final world. That is a third trigger and the version note in `replay.ts` now says so. `refuses a link recorded by the build before this one` pins it with a real version 1 string (`1.7.8.6.3.24.s~`) rather than `REPLAY_VERSION - 1`, because a derived version cannot tell a bumped build from an unbumped one.
  - **`replayRefusalIn`'s turn bound is now `setup.maxTurns` and the old `LIMITS.maxGameTurns` bound was removed, not added to.** It is subsumed: `deadline_too_long` runs first and already guarantees `setup.maxTurns <= LIMITS.maxGameTurns`, so the global cap could never fire after it and keeping both would have left an unreachable branch. `playback.ts`'s own `too_many_turns` guard stays at `LIMITS.maxGameTurns` - it bounds its own playback loop rather than the replay's honesty, and M3.9's "each bound is enforced by whoever owns it" says they are different bounds.
  - **The AC's "through real play" is `SHORT_SETUP` in `turn.test.ts`**: a generated map, a real hunt, and a deadline of 2 turns, over `SEED` and all seven `ESCAPE_SEEDS`. Two is measured rather than assumed - generation refuses a start adjacent to an exit (DESIGN.md "Generation validity") so one turn is provably too few, the quickest escape across `ESCAPE_SEEDS` is turn 4, and the quickest across a 2000-seed sweep is turn 3. A passive hunter spends no trust and two turns cannot reach the casualty threshold, so the clock is the only thing that can end those hunts, and the test also asserts the criminal is not standing on an exit when it does.
  - **Nothing new is exported.** `GameOutcome` and `EndConditionKind` were already public from `core/src/index.ts`, so the new variant reaches `web` and `sim` through the types they already import; `index.ts` is untouched.
  - **What M4.2b must know.** Aggregating by `outcome.kind` now has five buckets plus `in_progress`, and `in_progress` is no longer reachable at the end of a hunt that ran to its deadline - the 75 of 200 greedy hunts M4.1 measured as still running will come back `timed_out`. A batch that still sees `in_progress` has a bug in its own loop, not in `core`, which makes "every hunt counted in exactly one outcome bucket" an assertion the runner can actually make. Scores from that batch will move: those 75 hunts go from 0 to 150 plus whatever trust survived, so M4.1's "playing is currently punished" measurement is now out of date on the greedy side and M4.3 should re-measure rather than read it.
  - **Mutation check: 9 mutants, 9 killed, one survivor found and fixed.** Killed - the deadline comparison `>=` to `>` (8 tests), the deadline read off a fixed 24 instead of `world.config.maxTurns` (3), the `timed_out` entry deleted from `END_CONDITIONS` (7), that entry moved to the head of the table (10), `outcomeBase.timed_out` zeroed (4), raised to 600 past the invariant (6), the replay bound reverted to `LIMITS.maxGameTurns` (8), and the replay bound made off-by-one (8). **The survivor was `REPLAY_VERSION` reverted to 1**, which the whole suite missed because M3.9's literals had all been rewritten as `${REPLAY_VERSION}` templates and every version test was relative; the hard-coded version 1 link above was written to kill it, and does.
  - `endconditions.ts`, `replay.ts`, `balance.ts`, `world.ts` and `score.ts` are all at 100% statements, branches, functions and lines. `core` overall is 99.6% statements / 96.68% branches, up from 99.59 / 96.68.
  - `bun run verify` passes in 12s; 801 tests (was 770) in 45 files (was 43 - M5.2 added two, this task added none), 113 files checked by Biome, zero lint output.

- [x] **M4.2b Batch runner and aggregation**
  deps: M4.2a
  Play N hunts from a seed sequence with one of M4.1's bots and fold them into one serializable report: escape rate, capture rate, avg turns, loss reasons, and the worst/best seeds. Logic only - no argv, no filesystem, no `console`; M4.2c owns every boundary.
  `sim` may `unseal` (see "How `sim` reaches inside a sealed world"), and this is the consumer that decision named. M4.1's bots may not, and the `biome.json` override that stops them is scoped to `packages/sim/src/bots/**` - keep the runner outside that directory.
  Bounded by `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun`, which is still the M0.2 placeholder; this task tunes it on a measurement, the way M2.3 tuned `maxMapGenerationAttempts`.
  AC: 1,000 games run in reasonable time and the elapsed figure is in the task note; every hunt played is counted in exactly one outcome bucket, asserted rather than assumed; the report round-trips through JSON; a batch one over `maxGamesPerRun` is an error result, not a clamp.

  Notes:
  - **The report is a fold, not a pass over a list of results.** A run may be `maxGamesPerRun` hunts long and a finished hunt is not small - it carries its own replay - so `batch.ts` folds each hunt as it ends into a `BatchAccumulator` of counts, two running sums and the two hunts singled out so far. A run's memory is therefore flat in the number of games (measured below), and `runner.ts` never holds more than one world. `batch.ts` is bare functions over plain data rather than a `create*Logic` factory, because it has no collaborators at all - the `outcomeAfter` and `makeReplay` precedent in `core`.
  - **Rates and averages divide by `played`, never by `games`.** `games` is every seed attempted and `played` is the hunts that started; a setup whose maps cannot be generated says so in `notStarted` instead of quietly diluting every rate beside it. `leaves no attempted hunt out of the denominator` asserts `played + sum(notStarted) === games` rather than trusting it.
  - **Loss reasons are the `outcomes` record, not their own fields.** `outcomes` is a total `Record<GameOutcome["kind"], number>` - every ending counted, the losses being the losing rows - so the AC's "loss reasons" needs no second table and M4.3 reads trust collapses and casualties the same way it reads escapes. Only `escapeRate` and `captureRate` are named as rates, because those two are what the AC and M4.3's targets speak in. Both count tables (`NO_OUTCOMES`, `NO_STARTS`) are written out in full rather than derived from their key type: a total record literal is what makes a seventh `GameOutcome` variant or a third `GameResult` refusal a compile error here, and `Object.fromEntries` would build the same table and lose that.
  - **Decision: a hunt that never started is counted, and counted apart.** `HuntRecord` is a union - `played` or `not_started` - and `not_started` carries `reason: Exclude<GameResult["kind"], "game">`, so `deadline_too_long` and `generation_failed` come from `core`'s own refusal kinds and a new one is a compile error rather than a hunt that vanishes from the denominator. Such a hunt is in `games` and in `notStarted`, never in `outcomes`: it played no turns, so it has no turns, no score and no replay, and counting it as one of the endings would say the hunt ran and ended that way. Two tests drive real refusals through the runner (a 1000-turn deadline, and a 3x3 grid no river can cross).
  - **Decision: the runner builds a `Replay` for every hunt and the report keeps two.** M3.9 named "M5.2's store and M4.2's runner" as the two places that would record one, so the runner records. Keeping all of them would make memory linear in `games`, so `best` and `worst` carry theirs and the rest are dropped with the accumulator frame - M4.2c gets a shareable link for exactly the two hunts anyone would look at. A queue is pushed only after the `step` that accepted it, so a refused turn leaves no trace and the recording is of the hunt that happened; `records nothing from a turn the step refused` pins that with an injected bot that over-queues.
  - **Decision: `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun` is 10_000, down from M0.2's placeholder 100_000, tuned on measurement the way M2.3 tuned `maxMapGenerationAttempts`.** Time is the only constraint and memory is not: a 1000-game batch and a 100-game batch both stay under 12 MB of heap and produce a report of about 1.6 KB, because the fold keeps two hunts whatever the batch size. What a batch costs is wall clock - about 8 ms a game on the default 8x6 grid at a 24-turn deadline, and about 280 ms a game on the widest legal one (16x16 at `core`'s 240-turn `maxGameTurns`). At 100_000 those are 13 minutes and 8 hours; at 10_000 they are 80 seconds and 47 minutes. A cap is what a mistyped `--games` runs into, so it is set where the worst legal config still finishes inside an hour, with an order of magnitude over the largest batch the plan asks for.
  - **`BatchRequest.maxGames` overrides that cap, for the same reason `ArtifactRequest.maxBytes` does (M2.4):** a test that has to stand on both sides of the bound should not have to play ten thousand hunts to reach it. The real limit still has its own over-the-limit test at `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun + 1`, and refusal is an error result carrying both numbers, checked before a single hunt is created rather than after `maxGamesPerRun` of them.
  - **The runner is the consumer the "How `sim` reaches inside a sealed world" decision named.** A finished hunt is read through `unseal` for its outcome and its clock. Both are in `HunterView` too, but projecting a whole view - belief, reports, events - to read two numbers off it, once per game and up to `maxGamesPerRun` times, is work for nothing. It lives at `packages/sim/src/runner.ts`, outside the `packages/sim/src/bots/**` that `biome.json` denies `unseal` to, which is what M4.1 left the directory boundary for. `turns` is the clock rather than `outcome.turn`: for a settled hunt M3.8c makes them the same number, and the clock is also right for a hunt left unsettled, which the stamp cannot be because there is none.
  - **Structure is asserted; no distribution is.** M3.11 moves the RNG stream, so every seed plays a different hunt from this commit on, and pinning win rates is M4.3's job with its own hard-coded seed list. What `runner.test.ts` pins instead is that each of the three bots counts every seed exactly once, that `in_progress` is empty (M4.2a made the deadline an ending, so a batch that still sees one has a bug in its own loop), that a 3-game batch equals its three 1-game batches hunt for hunt, that the named hunt's replay plays back to the same outcome and total, that the same run reports the same twice, and that the report round-trips through JSON. `batch.test.ts` pins the fold's arithmetic over hand-built records, where a distribution cannot get in.
  - **Mutation check: 27 mutants, 25 killed, 2 survivors, both equivalent.** 22 fell on the first pass; the four that did not were fixed by one new test and one new assertion rather than argued away. `records nothing from a turn the step refused` kills pushing the queue before the step, and `in_progress` being zero in the per-bot test kills a loop that stops after one turn - which no other assertion noticed, since a one-turn hunt is still internally consistent. The two that remain are equivalent: forking the bot's stream off the game seed versus reusing it changes which hunts play and nothing structural, which is exactly what this task may not assert; and deleting the `isHuntOver` guard changes nothing observable, because `step` refuses a settled world with `hunt_over` and the loop returns on that anyway - the guard saves a bot decision, it does not change a record.
  - **Measured, 1000 games a bot on the default 8x6 grid at a 24-turn deadline (the AC's elapsed figure): 8.5s for `random`, 8.0s for `greedy_roadblock`, 7.5s for `heatmap_chaser`** - 7.5-8.5 ms a hunt, taken while a second agent was running its own suite on the same box, so they are an upper bound rather than a best case. Heap stayed under 12 MB and each report serialised to 1.3-1.6 KB. The widest legal config (16x16 at the 240-turn `maxGameTurns`) costs 274-288 ms a hunt; `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun` carries both numbers.
  - **The figures were taken with M3.11 in the tree, not before it, but M4.3 must still re-measure.** M3.11 was landing in `core` while this task ran. Where the three bots stand today over 1000 hunts each: `random` captures 87 and loses 905 to escapes, 5 to the clock and 3 to casualties (avg 5.1 turns, avg score 105); `greedy_roadblock` captures 695 and loses 210 to escapes, 68 to the clock and 27 to casualties (avg 7.1 turns, avg score 813); `heatmap_chaser` captures none and escapes 999 (avg 4.6 turns, avg score 14). M4.1's "playing is punished" measurement is superseded. M4.3's targets - random 5-25%, greedy not over 70% - are close on two bots and missed by `heatmap_chaser`, but they must be re-derived from its own hard-coded seed list rather than read off these.
  - **No hunt in any of those 3000 came back `in_progress`**, which is what M4.2a's deadline ending promised M4.2b, so the per-bot test asserts it: a batch that still sees one has a bug in its own loop.
  - `bun run verify` passes with zero warnings: 47 files, 843 tests, about 12s in the test step. The two new test files add 24 of those (9 in `batch.test.ts`, 15 in `runner.test.ts`); the `sim` project is 7 files and 69 tests and runs in about 1.5s.
  - **What M4.2c needs.** `createRunnerLogic({ rng, game, turn, bots, scoring })` at `main.ts`, beside the artefact writer M2.4 left there; every one of those five is already constructed in `bots.test.ts`'s wiring block, which is the shape of the composition root. Then `run({ bot, setup, seed, games, balance })` returns `{ kind: "report" }` or `{ kind: "too_many_games", requestedGames, maxGames }` - the second is the `--games` refusal the M4.2c AC asks for, already shaped as an error result rather than a clamp, so the CLI needs to check `maxCliArguments` and the flag grammar and nothing else about batch size. `BatchReport` is plain JSON for the `--json` output; the human-readable one wants `games`, `played`, `notStarted`, `outcomes`, the two rates, the two averages and `best`/`worst` with `encodeReplay(best.replay)` as the link. `run` never throws and never writes.

- [x] **M4.2c CLI surface**
  deps: M4.2b
  `bun run sim -- --games N --bot X --difficulty Y --seed S [--out dir]`, wired in `packages/sim/src/main.ts`, which M2.4 left a no-op for exactly this. JSON and human-readable output.
  **`--difficulty`, not the `--profile` in AGENTS.md's command table.** `GameSetup` carries a difficulty and the criminal profile is derived from the seed and hidden (M3.1a's config/setup split, and DESIGN.md "the player never picks a profile"), so there is no profile for a flag to set. The table predates that split; there is an Inbox entry asking the maintainer to correct it.
  Owns a real I/O boundary: AGENTS.md section 5 makes argv bounded input, so the argument count is checked against `LIMITS.maxCliArguments` and `--games` against `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun` before anything is allocated. Reuses M2.4's `createArtifactWriter` and `createFileSink` for `--out` rather than writing to a disk itself, and wires `createMapSvgLogic` there too, as M2.4's Inbox entry asked.
  AC: JSON + human-readable output; `--games` one over the limit is an error result, not a clamp; an argv one over `maxCliArguments` is refused before parsing; an unknown flag, an unknown bot and a missing value are each a named error rather than a throw.

  Notes:
  - **The grammar is `--flag value` pairs and nothing else: no switches, no `--help`, no positional arguments.** Every flag in `FLAGS` takes a value, so argv splits into pairs before anything is interpreted and a trailing flag is the single shape the split can refuse; that is what keeps the parser a pair split plus one table lookup, at nesting depth 1 and well under the cognitive-complexity cap, instead of the `if`-chain a flag parser usually becomes. A switch flag would need a second column in `FLAG_TABLE` and buys nothing the grammar has a use for. Usage is printed on every refusal rather than behind a `--help` nobody in a batch script types.
  - **`--difficulty`, never `--profile`, and the flag is the whole of the setup a run can name.** `GameSetup` carries a difficulty; the criminal profile is derived from the seed and hidden (M3.1a, DESIGN.md "the player never picks a profile"), so there is nothing for a `--profile` flag to set. The AGENTS.md command table still shows `--profile amateur`; that correction is the maintainer's existing Inbox entry and was left alone here.
  - **Decision: the grid and the deadline come from `balance`, not from flags.** `setupFrom` builds `{ map: balance.map.defaults, maxTurns: balance.time.maxTurns, difficulty: options.difficulty }`. A `--columns` or `--max-turns` flag would be a second place to state numbers `core/src/balance.ts` already owns (architecture rule 7). **M4.3 consequence: every figure a run prints is a figure about today's `BALANCE`**, so a target measured now moves the moment M4.3 turns a balance knob, and a target that needs a different grid needs a balance change rather than a flag.
  - **Decision: seeds are a contiguous range, `seed .. seed + games - 1`, and the text report prints it as `seeds: S-E`.** That is M4.2b's `BatchRequest` shape surfaced, not a new idea. **M4.3 consequence: a hard-coded, non-contiguous seed list cannot be handed to the CLI today.** M4.3 either pins a range (one run) or a list (one run per seed, or a new `--seeds` flag it adds itself with its own bound). Nothing in this task guesses which.
  - **Decision: `--format json` prints `BatchReport` verbatim, so M4.3 can parse stdout rather than scrape the text.** `toBatchJson` is `JSON.stringify(report)` with no wire shape invented on top, which means `JSON.parse(stdout)` gives back exactly the type M4.2b's fold produced: `games`, `played`, `notStarted`, `outcomes` (total over every `GameOutcome` kind), `captureRate`, `escapeRate`, `averageTurns`, `averageScore`, and `best`/`worst` with their replays. The text format is the same data for a person, with the two rates as `NN.N%` and the averages to two decimals, and it leads with `bot:` and `difficulty:` because `BatchReport` does not carry the request and a report with no bot name on it cannot be compared with the one beside it.
  - **Decision: `--games` is bounded by the runner, not by the CLI.** M4.2b already refuses an oversized batch before it creates a hunt and returns both numbers, so the CLI parses `--games` without a ceiling and surfaces `too_many_games` as a message; a second copy of `LIMITS.maxGamesPerRun` in the parser would be a second number to keep in step. One over the limit is therefore still an error result and never a clamp, and it is refused in milliseconds because no hunt is created.
  - **The new I/O boundary this task owns is argv, and it is bounded in two dimensions before a single argument is read.** `LIMITS.maxCliArguments` (32, unchanged) bounds how many arrive and the new `LIMITS.maxCliArgumentLength` (4096) bounds how large one may be; a count alone leaves argv unbounded in bytes, which is not what AGENTS.md section 5 asks for. 4096 is the POSIX `PATH_MAX`, sized on `--out`'s directory, the longest argument the grammar has a use for. Both have a test exactly at the bound and one over it, and the over-the-limit argv deliberately also carries an unknown trailing flag, so a parser that checked the bound after reading would name the wrong mistake and fail.
  - **Nothing in the CLI throws on user error.** Ten refusal kinds - `too_many_arguments`, `argument_too_long`, `unknown_flag`, `missing_value`, `empty_value`, `invalid_number`, `unknown_bot`, `unknown_difficulty`, `unknown_format` and the options case - are a `kind` union, each carrying what its message needs so `output.ts` formats data rather than re-deriving it. A filesystem that rejects a write is caught at the app boundary and turned into the same kind of result, because a mistyped `--out` is a user's mistake and not a bug. Exceptions are left for bugs.
  - **Decision: `app.ts` returns `{ status, lines }`; `main.ts` owns the only `console`, the only `process` and the exit code.** No logger is injected, because a run's output is data until the moment it is printed, and a test that reads the whole output as an array is worth more than one that captures a stream. `status: "failed"` becomes exit code 1 - a bad flag, an oversized batch, or any artefact that did not get written - so **M4.3's scripts can trust the exit code** rather than grepping stdout.
  - **Decision: `--out DIR` writes two artefacts, `batch-<bot>-<seed>.json` and `map-<seed>.svg`, and the report file is JSON whatever `--format` printed.** A file is read by a program. This is where M2.4's Inbox entry is finally honoured: `createMapSvgLogic` is wired at `main.ts` beside `createArtifactWriter({ sink: createFileSink() })`, and the map is the first seed's own city, which costs one extra generation of a map the runner already built and dropped - noise beside a batch, and cheaper than threading a graph out of the fold. **`--out` does not create the directory**: a missing one is an ENOENT reported as a named write failure and a non-zero exit, which is an Inbox entry below rather than a decision taken here.
  - **Mutation check: 43 mutants, 41 killed, 2 survivors, both equivalent by design.** 39 fell on the first pass. The two that did not were test weaknesses and were fixed rather than argued away: `names every flag in the grammar` passed even with the `--out` usage line deleted, because the header line joins `FLAG_NAMES` and already contains every flag (now `gives every flag in the grammar a line of its own`, asserting a line that starts with the flag); and the oversized-artefact test asserted its two numbers against the whole output, where a 100-game report contains every digit somewhere (now asserted against the write lines alone, with distinctive sizes). The two survivors are both "the test reads the named constant instead of restating its value": changing `DEFAULT_GAMES` from 100 to 50, and changing `LIMITS.maxCliArguments` from 32 to 64. Both are deliberate - AGENTS.md puts limits and defaults in named constants, and the tests pin behaviour *at* the bound and *against* `DEFAULT_OPTIONS`, so restating the literals would only duplicate them.
  - `bun run verify` passes with zero warnings: 50 files, 909 tests, about 11s in the test step. This task adds 66 of those - 25 in `cli.test.ts`, 25 in `output.test.ts`, 16 in `app.test.ts` - and the `sim` project is now 10 files and 135 tests in about 1.6s. Coverage over the files this task wrote, measured with a one-off root config (see Inbox): `output.ts` and `app.ts` are 100% of statements and branches, `cli.ts` is 100% of statements and 96.9% of branches - the one uncovered branch is the `argv[index] ?? ""` that `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` forces on an index the loop already bounds - and `main.ts` is 0%, which is the composition root and is covered by running the binary rather than by a test.
  - **Measured end to end, 100 games a bot on the default 8x6 grid at a 24-turn deadline, seed 1, including Bun start-up: 864ms `random`, 882ms `greedy_roadblock`, 841ms `heatmap_chaser`** - about 8.5ms a hunt, matching M4.2b's own figure, so `--games 1000` is under 10 seconds and the 10_000 cap is about 85. Where the three bots stand on that exact run: `random` captures 9.0% at an average score of 106.62, `greedy_roadblock` 76.0% at 890.36, `heatmap_chaser` 0.0% at 12.96. **These are one seed range and are not targets**; M4.3 must re-derive its own from its own seeds, and note that `greedy_roadblock` is already over the "not over 70%" figure the plan sketches.
  - **What M4.3 needs to drive a run.** `bun run sim -- --games N --bot X --difficulty Y --seed S [--format json] [--out DIR]`, defaults `--games 100 --bot greedy_roadblock --difficulty standard --seed 1 --format text`, no required flag, later flag wins over earlier, exit code 0 or 1. For a balance sweep the cheapest surface is `--format json` on stdout; for an artefact trail, `--out`. Nothing in `sim` reads the clock or the environment, so a run with the same flags is byte-identical, which is what lets M4.3 hard-code expected numbers at all.

- [x] **M4.3 Balance targets**
  deps: M4.2c, M3.11
  Add a test that runs a small batch and asserts rough targets (e.g. random bot wins 5–25%, greedy bot does not win > 70%). Tune `balance.ts`.
  Same rule as M3.6: a hard-coded seed list and a named batch size, never a generated one. A balance target that drifts between runs of the same commit is not a target.
  **M3.11 is a dep because without it no bot can win at all** and the targets above could not be met by tuning any knob (Inbox, and the capture decision below). It also invalidates every measurement M4.1 and M4.2b record: re-measure rather than tuning against the numbers in those notes.
  Three Inbox entries land here as the things to check the tuning against, not just the win rate: a hunt that lasts more than five turns, losses that rank against each other instead of all flooring at `minimumScore`, and a hunter who plays outscoring one who does nothing.
  M4.2a set `outcomeBase.timed_out` to a deliberately provisional 150 and M3.11 set `actions.roadblock.slipPastChance` to 0.6 the same way; both are this task's to tune. The slip-past chance is the one knob that moves the win rate directly, and M3.11 measured it rather than deriving it (greedy-on-belief captures 63% at 0.6, 75% at 0.4, 50% at 0.7, over 500 generated hunts); sweep it against the bot suite rather than treating those three points as the curve.
  AC: targets documented in `balance.ts` comments; test passes; the batch size and seed list are named constants.

  Notes:
  - **The targets are four bands in `packages/sim/src/balance.slow.test.ts`, measured over 400 hunts from four disjoint hard-coded seed blocks (`TARGET_SEED_BLOCKS = [1, 5001, 90001, 1000001]`, `GAMES_PER_BLOCK = 100`) on the default 8x6 grid at the 24-turn deadline.** What the shipped balance actually reads there: `greedy_roadblock` captures 60.3% in a mean 8.56 turns for a mean score of 695.2; `random` captures 7.2% in 5.18 turns for 87.3; a hunter who does nothing captures 0% in 4.65 turns for 51.8; `heatmap_chaser` captures 0% in 4.64 turns for 14.2. Four blocks rather than one contiguous run of 400 so that a single unlucky region of the seed space cannot carry a band, and both numbers are named constants - a target that drifts between runs of the same commit is not a target.
  - **Decision: `actions.roadblock.slipPastChance` is 0.7, up from M3.11's 0.6, and the window around it was measured from both edges rather than judged.** Worked-hunt capture across the sweep: 79.3% at 0.5, 75.8% at 0.55, 71.8% at 0.58, 70.0% at 0.6, 66.3% at 0.65, 62.7% at 0.68, 60.3% at 0.7, 56.5% at 0.72, 51.7% at 0.75, 47.5% at 0.78, 43.3% at 0.8, 36.5% at 0.85. The targets therefore fail at 0.58 (over the ceiling) and at 0.78 (under the floor), so [0.60, 0.75] is the passing window. 0.7 is not the middle of that window but the middle of the band it is judged by: 60% is halfway between the 50% floor and the 70% ceiling, and 0.7 is where the curve crosses it. M3.11's 0.6 sat exactly on the ceiling with zero margin, which is why it moved.
  - **Which target bites and which is a guard rail - read this before retuning anything.** Only `takes the criminal in most hunts a hunter works, and loses three in ten` brackets the knob from both sides; `runs a worked hunt well past the five turns an unworked one takes` bites below 0.55 (worked hunts fall to 5.62 turns at 0.5 against the unworked 4.65); `does not punish a hunter for spending on a plan that is only half a plan` bites at 0.85 (aimless mean 49.6 against idle's 51.8); `lets a hunter with no plan win sometimes, and rarely` never fires anywhere in 0 to 0.85 (11.5% down to 4.0%, inside [2%, 20%] the whole way) and only a checkpoint nobody can ever be taken at puts it out. The wide bands were left wide deliberately: they state what the game may not become, and narrowing them onto the measured value would be fitting the target to the run.
  - **Decision: M3.11's `takes the criminal on every seed the passive hunter loses` was narrowed to what M3.11's acceptance criterion actually promised, and the behaviour that broke it was promoted into a test of its own rather than deleted.** M3.11 promised that capture is reachable through real play, demonstrated on a hard-coded seed; that is an existence proof and needs one seed, not seven. The seven-of-seven assertion was a stronger claim than the balance ever made, and at 0.7 seed 7 stopped satisfying it.
  - **The evidence that seven-of-seven was a coincidence, not a property.** The omniscient blockader in `turn.test.ts` captures 82.5% of seeds 1-200 at 0.6, so seven independent seeds all landing `captured` was a roughly 27% event at the setting it was written under. The curve has no knee to anchor on either: captures over those 200 seeds run 165, 155, 138, 120 at slip 0.6, 0.65, 0.7, 0.75, degrading smoothly, and individual seeds move all over the range (seed 3 already goes from 3 turns to 5 at slip 0.5; seed 1 falls out at 0.8). "All seven inside 24 turns" cannot pick a number, so it could not be used to reject 0.7.
  - **The evidence that 0.7 is not too far: at 0.7 the omniscient hunter does not lose, it runs out of clock.** Over seeds 1-200 the blockader concedes exactly one escape at 0.6, 0.65, 0.7 and 0.75 alike - the corridor holds identically at every setting. What the knob moves is how long the hold lasts (mean 5.91, 7.13, 8.96, 10.96 turns) and how often the deadline beats the arrest (captures 165/155/138/120, stalemates 29/38/54/71). Seed 7, the seed that went red, ends `timed_out` at turn 24 with the criminal neither in custody nor standing on an exit: contained for the entire hunt. The second reading offered for this decision - an omniscient hunter failing to capture means 0.7 is too far - measures the wrong thing, because the hunter is not failing and the escape rate does not move.
  - **What the three `turn.test.ts` tests now prove, and why that is not a weakening.** `ends a hunt the hunter works the checkpoints in, with the criminal taken` still discharges M3.11 through real play on a hard-coded seed, now `TAKEN_SEED = 1` - the first entry of `ESCAPE_SEEDS`, not a seed searched for. `holds a criminal it cannot take until the deadline runs out` is new and runs the seed that went red (`CONTAINED_SEED = SEED = 7`), asserting the full 24 turns, `timed_out`, not in custody, and not on an exit. `stops the escape on every seed the passive hunter loses, and takes most of them` keeps the seven-seed loop and asserts the two things the balance does claim: nobody walks out past a closed corridor (true on all seven at every slip from 0 to 0.9), and at least `ceil(7/2)` are taken, which is the slow project's own 50% floor applied to seven hunts. Nothing was dropped - assertion count went up, the red behaviour is now pinned explicitly instead of being averaged into a band, and the suite's bite on the knob went from "0.6 or below" to [0.70, 0.75].
  - **Consequence for M5/M7: the two `core` tests above are knob-sensitive on purpose.** Moving `slipPastChance` outside [0.70, 0.75] turns `turn.test.ts` red before the slow project ever runs. That is the design: a captured hunt and a contained hunt are the two outcomes this knob trades against each other, so a change to the trade has to say so out loud.
